A <a href="/wiki/2008_Glasgow_East_by-election" title="2008 Glasgow East by-election">by-election in Glasgow East</a> triggered by the resignation of <a href="/wiki/David_Marshall_(British_politician)" title="David Marshall (British politician)">David Marshall</a> saw Labour struggle to appoint a candidate, eventually settling for <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Curran" title="Margaret Curran">Margaret Curran</a>, a sitting MSP in the <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Parliament" title="Scottish Parliament">Scottish Parliament</a>. The SNP, Conservatives and Liberal Democrats all derided Labour for their disorganised nature, with <a href="/wiki/Alex_Salmond" title="Alex Salmond">Alex Salmond</a> commenting "This is their 'lost weekend'—they don't have a leader in Scotland, they don't have a candidate in Glasgow East, and they have a prime minister who refuses to come to the constituency".<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Labour lost the constituency to the <a href="/wiki/Scottish_National_Party" title="Scottish National Party">Scottish National Party</a>'s <a href="/wiki/John_Mason_(Scottish_politician)" title="John Mason (Scottish politician)">John Mason</a> who took 11,277 votes, with Labour just 365 behind. The seat experienced a swing of 22.54%.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/2009_European_Parliament_election_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="2009 European Parliament election in the United Kingdom">European elections</a>, Labour polled 16% of the vote, finishing in third place behind the Conservatives and <a href="/wiki/UK_Independence_Party" title="UK Independence Party">UK Independence Party</a> (UKIP).<sup id="cite_ref-labour_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-labour-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Voter apathy was reflected in the historically low turnout of around thirty-three per cent. In Scotland, voter turnout was only twenty-eight per cent. In the <a href="/wiki/2009_United_Kingdom_local_elections" title="2009 United Kingdom local elections">local elections</a>, Labour polled 23% of the vote, finishing in third place behind <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservatives</a> and <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_(UK)" title="Liberal Democrats (UK)">Liberal Democrats</a>, with Labour losing control of the four councils it had held prior to the election.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> In a vote widely considered to be a reaction to the expenses scandal, the share of the votes was down for all the major parties; Labour was down one per cent, the Conservative share was down five per cent. The beneficiary of the public backlash was generally seen to be the minor parties, including the <a href="/wiki/Green_Party_of_England_and_Wales" title="Green Party of England and Wales">Green Party</a> and UKIP. These results were Labour's worst since World War II. Brown was quoted in the press as having said that the results were "a painful defeat for Labour", and that "too many good people doing so much good for their communities and their constituencies have lost through no fault of their own."<sup id="cite_ref-timesonline1_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-timesonline1-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2010_general_election">2010 general election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: 2010 general election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2010_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="2010 United Kingdom general election">2010 United Kingdom general election</a></div>
<p>In April 2010, Brown asked the Queen to dissolve Parliament. The general election campaign included the first televised leadership debates in Britain. The result of the election on 6 May was a <a href="/wiki/Hung_parliament" title="Hung parliament">hung parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_07-05-2010_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_07-05-2010-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Brown was re-elected as MP for Kirkcaldy and Cowdenbeath with 29,559 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_07-05-2010_135-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_07-05-2010-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2010_government_formation_and_resignation">2010 government formation and resignation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: 2010 government formation and resignation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2010_United_Kingdom_government_formation" title="2010 United Kingdom government formation">2010 United Kingdom government formation</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/2010_Labour_Party_leadership_election_(UK)" title="2010 Labour Party leadership election (UK)">2010 Labour Party leadership election (UK)</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Queen_and_Gordon_Brown_11_May_2010_(7141493275).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/The_Queen_and_Gordon_Brown_11_May_2010_%287141493275%29.jpg/220px-The_Queen_and_Gordon_Brown_11_May_2010_%287141493275%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/The_Queen_and_Gordon_Brown_11_May_2010_%287141493275%29.jpg/330px-The_Queen_and_Gordon_Brown_11_May_2010_%287141493275%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/The_Queen_and_Gordon_Brown_11_May_2010_%287141493275%29.jpg/440px-The_Queen_and_Gordon_Brown_11_May_2010_%287141493275%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="511" data-file-height="378" /></a><figcaption>Brown tendering his resignation to <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Queen Elizabeth II</a> at Buckingham Palace</figcaption></figure>
<p>Brown announced on 10 May 2010 that he would stand down as Labour Leader, with a view to a successor being chosen before the next <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_Conference" title="Labour Party Conference">Labour Party Conference</a> in September 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-resignation_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-resignation-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> The following day, negotiations between the Labour Party and the Liberal Democrats to form a coalition government failed. During the evening, Brown visited <a href="/wiki/Buckingham_Palace" title="Buckingham Palace">Buckingham Palace</a> to tender his resignation as Prime Minister to <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Queen Elizabeth&#160;II</a> and to recommend that she invite the Leader of the Opposition, <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a>, to form a government.<sup id="cite_ref-PMRes_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PMRes-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> He resigned as leader of the Labour Party with immediate effect.<sup id="cite_ref-PMRes_138-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PMRes-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-premiership">Post-premiership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Post-premiership">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Return_to_the_backbenches_.282010.E2.80.932015.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_the_backbenches_(2010–2015)">Return to the backbenches (2010–2015)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Return to the backbenches (2010–2015)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:British_PMs_2011.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/British_PMs_2011.png/220px-British_PMs_2011.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/British_PMs_2011.png/330px-British_PMs_2011.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/British_PMs_2011.png/440px-British_PMs_2011.png 2x" data-file-width="550" data-file-height="381" /></a><figcaption>Brown with former Prime Ministers <a href="/wiki/Tony_Blair" title="Tony Blair">Tony Blair</a> and Sir <a href="/wiki/John_Major" title="John Major">John Major</a>, Deputy Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Nick_Clegg" title="Nick Clegg">Nick Clegg</a> and Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a> during U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>'s address to Parliament</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 13 May 2010, in his first public appearance since leaving <a href="/wiki/10_Downing_Street" title="10 Downing Street">10 Downing Street</a>, two days after resigning as Prime Minister and Leader of the Labour Party, Brown confirmed he intended to stay on in Parliament, serving as a Labour <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbencher</a>, to serve the people of his <a href="/wiki/Kirkcaldy_and_Cowdenbeath_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Kirkcaldy and Cowdenbeath (UK Parliament constituency)">Kirkcaldy and Cowdenbeath constituency</a>. He continued to serve as the MP of Kirkcaldy and Cowdenbeath until 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC13May2010Backbencher_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC13May2010Backbencher-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Towards the end of May 2010, Brown began writing <i><a href="/wiki/Beyond_the_Crash" title="Beyond the Crash">Beyond the Crash</a></i>, completing it after 14 weeks. The book discusses the 2007–08 financial crisis and Brown's recommendations for future co-ordinated global action.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
He played a prominent role in the lead-up to, and the aftermath of, the <a href="/wiki/2014_Scottish_independence_referendum" title="2014 Scottish independence referendum">2014 Scottish independence referendum</a>, campaigning for Scotland to stay in the United Kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> "Our vision for the future of Scotland – yes a Scottish parliament for fairness, battling for equality across the UK", he told voters in an impassioned speech on the eve of polling. "But our vision is bigger than that – at every point, particularly through our membership of the UK, to fight for what is our dream, what is our demand. A world not of a separate state, but a world of social justice people can believe in. What kind of message does Scotland send to the world if, tomorrow, we said we are going to give up on sharing, we are going to smash our partnership, we are going to abandon co-operation and we are going to throw the idea of solidarity into the dust. This is not the Scotland I know and recognise."<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Accelerating_Infrastructure_Development_Gordon_Brown_(8412051140).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Accelerating_Infrastructure_Development_Gordon_Brown_%288412051140%29.jpg/139px-Accelerating_Infrastructure_Development_Gordon_Brown_%288412051140%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="139" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Accelerating_Infrastructure_Development_Gordon_Brown_%288412051140%29.jpg/209px-Accelerating_Infrastructure_Development_Gordon_Brown_%288412051140%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Accelerating_Infrastructure_Development_Gordon_Brown_%288412051140%29.jpg/278px-Accelerating_Infrastructure_Development_Gordon_Brown_%288412051140%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3400" data-file-height="5504" /></a><figcaption>Brown, as UN Special Envoy for Global Education, addresses the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Forum" title="World Economic Forum">World Economic Forum</a>, January 2013.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 1 December 2014, Brown announced that he would not be seeking re-election to parliament. He stood down at the general election in May 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="IMF_speculation">IMF speculation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: IMF speculation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In April 2011, media reports linked Brown with the role of managing director of the International Monetary Fund following the scheduled retirement of <a href="/wiki/Dominique_Strauss-Kahn" title="Dominique Strauss-Kahn">Dominique Strauss-Kahn</a>. Brown's successor and Leader of the Opposition, <a href="/wiki/Ed_Miliband" title="Ed Miliband">Ed Miliband</a>, supported Brown for the role, while the Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a>, voiced opposition to this.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Following the arrest of Strauss-Kahn for alleged sexual assault in May 2011, and his subsequent resignation, these reports re-surfaced.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> Support for Brown among economists was mixed but British Government backing for his candidature was not forthcoming and instead supported <a href="/wiki/Christine_Lagarde" title="Christine Lagarde">Christine Lagarde</a> – the eventual successful candidate – for the post.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_appointments">Other appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Other appointments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Boris_Johnson_with_Gordon_Brown_in_London_-_2018_(27295267767).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Boris_Johnson_with_Gordon_Brown_in_London_-_2018_%2827295267767%29.jpg/220px-Boris_Johnson_with_Gordon_Brown_in_London_-_2018_%2827295267767%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Boris_Johnson_with_Gordon_Brown_in_London_-_2018_%2827295267767%29.jpg/330px-Boris_Johnson_with_Gordon_Brown_in_London_-_2018_%2827295267767%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Boris_Johnson_with_Gordon_Brown_in_London_-_2018_%2827295267767%29.jpg/440px-Boris_Johnson_with_Gordon_Brown_in_London_-_2018_%2827295267767%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5472" data-file-height="3648" /></a><figcaption>Brown with Foreign Secretary <a href="/wiki/Boris_Johnson" title="Boris Johnson">Boris Johnson</a>, May 2018</figcaption></figure>
<p>Sir <a href="/wiki/Tim_Berners-Lee" title="Tim Berners-Lee">Tim Berners-Lee</a>, who had worked with the government during Brown's premiership to publish government data on the internet in the <a href="/wiki/Data.gov.uk" title="Data.gov.uk">data.gov.uk</a> project, subsequently invited Brown to become a board director of the <a href="/wiki/World_Wide_Web_Foundation" title="World Wide Web Foundation">World Wide Web Foundation</a> to "advise the Web Foundation on ways to involve disadvantaged communities and global leaders in the development of sustainable programs that connect humanity and affect positive change".<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> On 22 April 2011 it was announced that Brown would be taking on an unpaid advisory role at the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Forum" title="World Economic Forum">World Economic Forum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Brown was also appointed as the inaugural 'Distinguished Leader in Residence' by <a href="/wiki/New_York_University" title="New York University">New York University</a> and took part in discussions and lectures relating to the <a href="/wiki/2007%E2%80%932008_financial_crisis" title="2007–2008 financial crisis">global financial crisis</a><sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> and globalisation.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 2012 Brown was named by Secretary-General <a href="/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon" title="Ban Ki-moon">Ban Ki-moon</a> as a <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">United Nations</a> <a href="/wiki/Special_Envoy_of_the_Secretary-General" title="Special Envoy of the Secretary-General">Special Envoy</a> on Global Education. He chaired the <a href="/wiki/International_Commission_on_Financing_Global_Education_Opportunity" title="International Commission on Financing Global Education Opportunity">International Commission on Financing Global Education Opportunity</a>. The position is unpaid.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2015, Brown took his first large-scale role in the private sector since standing down as prime minister in 2010, becoming an advisor to <a href="/wiki/PIMCO" title="PIMCO">PIMCO</a>. Any money earned from the role is to go to the Gordon and Sarah Brown Foundation to support charitable work.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 7 November 2017, Brown released his memoir <i><a href="/wiki/My_Life,_Our_Times" title="My Life, Our Times">My Life, Our Times</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 2020, Brown was a co-author of a letter to the journal <i><a href="/wiki/Nature_(journal)" title="Nature (journal)">Nature</a></i> highlighting the importance of EU funding in the fight against <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic" title="COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> The letter was organised by <a href="/wiki/Scientists_for_Labour" title="Scientists for Labour">Scientists for Labour</a>, an organisation of which he is a patron.
</p><p>On 10 June 2021 Brown released the book <i>Seven Ways to Change the World: How To Fix The Most Pressing Problems We Face.</i> The book features Brown's forensic examination of seven areas where global reform and action are essential.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a former Prime Minister, Brown, with <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Jane_Brown" title="Sarah Jane Brown">Sarah</a>, had a place of honour at the <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Elizabeth_II" title="Death and state funeral of Elizabeth II">state funeral of Queen Elizabeth II</a> on 19 September 2022.
</p><p>On 5 December 2022, Brown and Labour leader <a href="/wiki/Keir_Starmer" title="Keir Starmer">Keir Starmer</a> unveiled a Labour Party blueprint for the future, proposing the biggest ever transfer of political power out of Westminster and into the towns, cities, and nations of the UK.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a former Prime Minister, Brown, with Sarah, had a place of honour at the <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_Charles_III_and_Camilla" title="Coronation of Charles III and Camilla">coronation of Charles III and Camilla</a> on 6 May 2023.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gordon_and_Sarah_Brown.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Gordon_and_Sarah_Brown.jpg/220px-Gordon_and_Sarah_Brown.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="149" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Gordon_and_Sarah_Brown.jpg/330px-Gordon_and_Sarah_Brown.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Gordon_and_Sarah_Brown.jpg/440px-Gordon_and_Sarah_Brown.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3448" data-file-height="2332" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Sarah_Jane_Brown" title="Sarah Jane Brown">Sarah Brown</a> attending one of her husband's speeches, September 2009</figcaption></figure>
<p>Brown's early girlfriends included journalist <a href="/wiki/Sheena_McDonald" title="Sheena McDonald">Sheena McDonald</a> and <a href="/wiki/Margareta_of_Romania" title="Margareta of Romania">Princess Margarita</a>, the eldest daughter of exiled <a href="/wiki/Michael_I_of_Romania" title="Michael I of Romania">King Michael of Romania</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-GBStory_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GBStory-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> At the age of 49, Brown married <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Jane_Brown" title="Sarah Jane Brown">Sarah Macaulay</a> in a private ceremony at his home in <a href="/wiki/North_Queensferry" title="North Queensferry">North Queensferry</a>, Fife, on 3 August 2000.<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> A daughter, Jennifer Jane, was born prematurely on 28 December 2001; she died on 7 January 2002, one day after experiencing a <a href="/wiki/Intracerebral_hemorrhage" title="Intracerebral hemorrhage">brain haemorrhage</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> The couple have two sons, John Macaulay (born 17 October 2003)<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> and (James) Fraser (born on 18 July 2006). In November 2006, Fraser was diagnosed with <a href="/wiki/Cystic_fibrosis" title="Cystic fibrosis">cystic fibrosis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sun_(United_Kingdom)" title="The Sun (United Kingdom)">The Sun</a></i> had learned of the situation in 2006 and published the story. In 2011, Brown stated he had wanted the details of his son's condition kept private and that the publication had left him "in tears".<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Sun</i> said they approached Brown and that discussion occurred with his colleagues who provided quotes to use in the article.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sarah Brown rarely made official appearances, whether with or without her husband.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> She is patron of several charities and has written articles for national newspapers related to this.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> At the 2008 Labour Party Conference, Sarah caused surprise by taking to the stage to introduce her husband for his keynote address.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Since then her public profile has increased.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_News_17_July_2009_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_News_17_July_2009-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The date of the event predicted near this tag has passed. (July 2020)">needs update</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Brown has two brothers, John Brown and <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Brown_(media_strategist)" title="Andrew Brown (media strategist)">Andrew Brown</a>. Andrew has been Head of Media Relations in the UK for the French-owned utility company <a href="/wiki/EDF_Energy" title="EDF Energy">EDF Energy</a> since 2004.<sup id="cite_ref-guardian.co.uk_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-guardian.co.uk-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> Brown is also the brother-in-law of environmental journalist <a href="/wiki/Clare_Rewcastle_Brown" title="Clare Rewcastle Brown">Clare Rewcastle Brown</a>; he wrote a piece for <i>The Independent</i> supporting Clare's current environmental efforts on behalf of <a href="/wiki/Sarawak" title="Sarawak">Sarawak</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Independent_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Independent-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While Prime Minister, Brown spent some of his spare time at <a href="/wiki/Chequers" title="Chequers">Chequers</a>, the house often being filled with friends. The Browns have entertained local dignitaries like Sir <a href="/wiki/Leonard_Figg" title="Leonard Figg">Leonard Figg</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Brown is also a friend of <a href="/wiki/Harry_Potter" title="Harry Potter">Harry Potter</a> author <a href="/wiki/J._K._Rowling" title="J. K. Rowling">J. K. Rowling</a>, who says of Brown: "I know him as affable, funny and gregarious, a great listener, a kind and loyal friend."<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brown is a strong supporter of the NHS, owing partly to both the experimental surgery that saved the sight in his right eye after his retina became detached, and the care he and Sarah Brown received when their premature firstborn baby died. It has been suggested that visual difficulties have contributed to Brown's supposed antisocial nature and awkward public manner. For example, both on a podium and before a camera, while reading "he needs to look slightly to one side of the paper to focus; when speaking to an audience or into a camera lens, he must remember to correct what would normally be an automatic tendency to look slightly askew to see clearly with his good eye". Brown's papers were prepared in capital letters and in extremely large type, resulting in his stack of papers at the dispatch box being noticeably bulky. Former staffers often attributed Brown's outbursts of temper in Downing Street to his frustration with his visual limitations. Nevertheless, it is noted that he has never allowed these limitations to hold him back and in fact attributed them to the shaping of his political character.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brown is a supporter of Kirkcaldy-based football club <a href="/wiki/Raith_Rovers_F.C." title="Raith Rovers F.C.">Raith Rovers</a> and has written articles about his relationship with the club.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>A son of a <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Scotland" title="Church of Scotland">Church of Scotland</a> <a href="/wiki/Minister_(Christianity)" title="Minister (Christianity)">minister</a>, Brown has talked about what he calls his "<a href="/wiki/Morality" title="Morality">moral compass</a>"<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> and of his parents being his "inspiration".<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> He has, at least ostensibly, been keen to keep his religion a private matter.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> According to <i>The Guardian</i>, he is a member of the Church of Scotland.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Depictions">Depictions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Depictions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><i><a href="/wiki/The_Deal_(2003_film)" title="The Deal (2003 film)">The Deal</a></i>, a 2003 television film, followed <a href="/wiki/Tony_Blair" title="Tony Blair">Tony Blair</a>'s rise to power, and his friendship and rivalry with Brown, played by <a href="/wiki/David_Morrissey" title="David Morrissey">David Morrissey</a>. In <i><a href="/wiki/The_Trial_of_Tony_Blair" title="The Trial of Tony Blair">The Trial of Tony Blair</a></i> (2007), Brown was played by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Mullan" title="Peter Mullan">Peter Mullan</a>, and in the <a href="/wiki/Channel_4" title="Channel 4">Channel 4</a> television film <i><a href="/wiki/Coalition_(film)" title="Coalition (film)">Coalition</a></i> (2015), he was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Ian_Grieve" title="Ian Grieve">Ian Grieve</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gordon_Brown&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gordon_Brown_at_University_of_Huddersfield.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Gordon_Brown_at_University_of_Huddersfield.jpg/220px-Gordon_Brown_at_University_of_Huddersfield.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Gordon_Brown_at_University_of_Huddersfield.jpg/330px-Gordon_Brown_at_University_of_Huddersfield.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Gordon_Brown_at_University_of_Huddersfield.jpg/440px-Gordon_Brown_at_University_of_Huddersfield.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3024" data-file-height="4032" /></a><figcaption>Brown delivers the <a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a> memorial lecture at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Huddersfield" title="University of Huddersfield">University of Huddersfield</a> in 2018.</figcaption></figure> <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctor of the University">Doctor of the University</a> (D.Univ.) from <a href="/wiki/Brunel_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Brunel University">Brunel University</a> (1996)<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Doctor_Honoris_Causa" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctor Honoris Causa">Doctor Honoris Causa</a> (Hon.Dr.) from <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh" title="University of Edinburgh">University of Edinburgh</a> (2003)<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Honorary <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctor of Laws">Doctor of Laws</a> (LL.D.) from <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gordon_University" title="Robert Gordon University">Robert Gordon University</a> (2003)<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Honorary <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Humane_Letters" title="Doctor of Humane Letters">Doctor of Humane Letters</a> (D.H.L.) from <a href="/wiki/New_York_University" title="New York University">New York University</a> (2005)<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Honorary <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Civil_Law" title="Doctor of Civil Law">Doctor of Civil Law</a> (D.C.L.) from <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_University" title="Newcastle University">Newcastle University</a> (2007)<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Honorary <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Letters" title="Doctor of Letters">Doctor of Letters</a> (D.Litt.) from <a href="/wiki/University_of_Delhi" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Delhi">University of Delhi</a> (2008)<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctor of the University">Doctor of the University</a> (D.Univ.) from <a href="/wiki/University_of_Glasgow" title="University of Glasgow">University of Glasgow</a> (2015)<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Elected an <a href="/wiki/Honorary_Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society_of_Edinburgh" class="mw-redirect" title="Honorary Fellow of the Royal Society of Edinburgh">Honorary Fellow of the Royal Society of Edinburgh</a> (HonFRSE; 2018)<sup id="cite_ref-HonFRSE_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HonFRSE-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>In March 2009, Brown was named <a href="/wiki/Appeal_of_Conscience_Foundation#World_Statesman_of_the_Year" title="Appeal of Conscience Foundation">World Statesman of the Year</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Appeal_of_Conscience_Foundation" title="Appeal of Conscience Foundation">Appeal of Conscience Foundation</a>, an American organisation 'dedicated to promoting peace, human rights and understanding between <a href="/wiki/Religion" title="Religion">religious faiths</a>'. The award was presented by <a href="/wiki/Rabbi" title="Rabbi">Rabbi</a> <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Schneier" title="Arthur Schneier">Arthur Schneier</a> who praised Brown's "compassionate leadership in dealing with the challenging issues facing humanity, his commitment to freedom, human dignity and the environment, and for the major role he has played in helping to stabilise the world's financial system".<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brown has, on six occasions, been honoured in the <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Politician_of_the_Year" title="Scottish Politician of the Year">Scottish Politician of the Year</a> awards organised by <i><a href="/wiki/The_Herald_(Glasgow)" title="The Herald (Glasgow)">The Herald</a></i> newspaper. In 1999, 2000, 2007 and 2014, he won the award in the Best Scot at Westminster category. He won the Lifetime Achievement Award/Outstanding Political Achievement category in 2011. And in 2020, he was designated "best of the best" in the Best Scot at Westminster category.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
